Poisson orders on large quantum groups Thanks: This material is based upon work supported by the National Science Foundation under Grant No. DMS-1440140 while N. A. was in residence at the Mathematical Sciences Research Institute in Berkeley, California, in the Spring 2020 semester. The work of N. A. and I. A. was partially supported by CONICET and Secyt (UNC). The work of M.Y was partially supported by NSF grants DMS-2131243 and DMS-2200762
Nicolás Andruskiewitsch, Iván Angiono, Milen Yakimov

Abstract
. We develop a Poisson geometric framework for studying the representation theory of all contragredient quantum super groups at roots of unity. This is done in a uniform fashion by treating the larger class of quantum doubles of bozonizations of all distinguished pre-Nichols algebras [ 9 ] belonging to a one-parameter family; we call these algebras large quantum groups. We prove that each of these quantum algebras has a central Hopf subalgebra giving rise to a Poisson order in the sense of [ 13 ] . We describe explicitly the underlying Poisson algebraic groups and Poisson homogeneous spaces in terms of Borel subgroups of complex semisimple algebraic groups of adjoint type. The geometry of the Poisson algebraic groups and Poisson homogeneous spaces that are involved and its applications to the irreducible representations of the algebras U 𝔮 ⊃ U 𝔮 ⩾ ⊃ U 𝔮 + U_{\mathfrak{q}}\supset U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}\supset U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+} are also described. Besides all (multiparameter) big quantum groups of De Concini–Kac–Procesi and big quantum super groups at roots of unity, our framework also contains the quantizations in characteristic 0 of the 34-dimensional Kac-Weisfeler Lie algebras in characteristic 2 and the 10-dimensional Brown Lie algebras in characteristic 3. The previous approaches to the above problems relied on reductions to rank two cases and direct calculations of Poisson brackets, which is not possible in the super case since there are 13 kinds of additional Serre relations on up to 4 generators. We use a new approach that relies on perfect pairings between restricted and non-restricted integral forms.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Quantum groups and Poisson orders

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra and let

\xi\in\mathbb{C}
be a root of 1 with some restrictions on its order depending on
\mathfrak{g}
.
In the papers
[
17
,
18
,
20
]
a quantized enveloping algebra

U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
at
\xi

was introduced and studied; it is a version of the Drinfeld-Jimbo quantized
universal enveloping algebra different from the one defined in
[
40
,
41
]
.

The algebra
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})

is module-finite over a central Hopf subalgebra
Z_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
and
the corresponding small quantum group of Lusztig
[
40
,
41
]
arises as the quotient

U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})/\hskip-3.0pt/Z_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
in the sense of Hopf algebras.
A geometric approach to the representation theory of
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
was proposed in

[
20
]
, based on these facts.
The key ingredients of this approach are:

\circ

The existence of a Poisson structure on
Z_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
so that the algebraic group
M
corresponding to this algebra is a Poisson algebraic group,
whose Lie bialgebra is dual to the standard Lie bialgebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}
.

\circ

The Hamiltonian vector fields on
M
extend to (explicit) derivations of
U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
.

The approach consists in packing the irreducible finite-dimensional representations of

U_{\xi}(\mathfrak{g})
along the symplectic leaves of
M
and predicting their dimensions.
These ideas were distilled in the notion of Poisson order in
[
13
]
, see Section
2
. The construction of a Poisson
order structure on an algebra has substantial applications to the representation theory of the algebra: using this route
the irreducible representations of quantum function algebras were studied in
[
19
]
,
the Azumaya loci of symplectic reflection algebras was described in
[
13
]
, the irreducible
representations of the 3 and 4-dimensional PI Sklyanin algebras were fully classified in
[
49
,
50
]
,
the Azumaya loci of the multiplicative quiver varieties and quantum character varieties were studied in

[
25
]
. See
[
14
, Part III]
for a comprehensive exposition of the applications
the notion of Poisson order to the representation theory of quantum algebras at roots of unity.

1.2.
Large quantum groups and pre-Nichols algebras

The main goal of this paper is to study by means of Poisson orders
the representation theory of a larger class of Hopf algebras
introduced by the second author in
[
8
]

and studied in
[
9
]
. They contain as special cases

\circ

all big quantum groups of De Concini–Kac–Procesi,

\circ

all big contragredient quantum super groups,

\circ

and exceptional families that can be viewed as quantizations of
the universal enveloping algebras of simple Lie algebras in positive characteristic.

The keystone of the definition of
these Hopf algebras is the notion of
distinguished pre-Nichols algebra
.
It allows us to treat all of the above families uniformly without case-by-case considerations and
computational arguments with quantum Serre relations.

Nichols algebras of diagonal type are essential for various classification problems of Hopf algebras.
Those of finite dimension were classified in the celebrated paper
[
30
]
while the defining relations
were provided in
[
7
,
8
]
.
Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a braiding matrix as in the list of
[
30
]
and let
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}

be the corresponding finite-dimensional Nichols algebra of diagonal type.
The distinguished pre-Nichols algebra
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}

of
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a covering of the latter defined by excluding
the powers of the root vectors of Cartan type from its defining ideal.
The Hopf algebras dealt with in the present paper are
Drinfeld doubles of the bosonizations of the distinguished pre-Nichols algebras; they are denoted

U_{\mathfrak{q}}
, see §
4.3
. They are shown to be module-finite over canonical central Hopf subalgebras
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
which are the ones defined in
[
9
]
if
\mathfrak{q}
is not of type super
A
and a one-dimensional extension of those in the
super
A
case, see §
4.5
.

On the other hand, the graded dual of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
gives rise to a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{q}}
, which is either 0 or the
nilpotent part of a semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}
that is explicitly determined
[
5
]
.

We focus on Hopf algebras
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
with a further restriction: the related Nichols algebra
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}

is deformable, i.e., belongs to a one-parameter family of Nichols algebras.
We call them
large quantum groups
. By inspection, the matrix
\mathfrak{q}
is of one of three types:

(a)

Cartan type (multiparameter versions of the quantum groups from
[
18
]
without restrictions on
\xi
);

(b)

super type (multiparameter quantum groups associated to finite dimensional simple contragredient Lie superalgebras at roots of unity);

(c)

modular types
\mathtt{wk}(4)
or
\mathtt{br}(2)
(quantizations at a root of unity
in characteristic 0 of some simple Lie algebras in characteristics 2 and 3 respectively).

But it stems from the list in
[
30
]
that
there are finite-dimensional Nichols algebras of diagonal type that do not belong to such one-parameter families.

Remark
.

To be precise we need three technical assumptions:

(i)

The base field is
\mathbb{C}
to have on hand symplectic leaves.
(For other algebraically closed fields of characteristic 0, one can use symplectic cores
[
13
]

and argue as in
[
49
, §6.4]
). See Appendix
C
for a brief discussion of symplecitc leaves and cores.

(ii)

Condition (
4.26
) is needed for the centrality of
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}
in
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

(iii)

The Non-degeneracy Assumption
7.5
is used to identify some dual vector spaces in order to
compute some Lie bialgebras. However this is not a constraint; each of the Hopf algebras
U_{\mathfrak{q}}

that we consider can be obtained as a specialization from a family in many different ways. We prove in Proposition

A.3
that there always exist
ways that satisfy Assumption
7.5
and choose one such way.

See the Appendix
A
and the survey
[
2
]
for full details on these algebras.
We consider the chain of subalgebras
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}\subset U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}\subset U_{\mathfrak{q}}
where

\circ

U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
, the
large quantum Borel subalgebra
, is identified with the bosonization of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
;

\circ

U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
, the
large quantum unipotent subalgebra
, is identified with
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

Intersecting the central subalgebra
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}
of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
gives the chain of central Hopf subalgebras

(1.1)

Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}\subset Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}\subset Z_{\mathfrak{q}}.

Each of these central Hopf subalgebras is actually isomorphic to a tensor product of a
polynomial algebra and a Laurent polynomial algebra. We introduce the algebraic groups

M_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
and
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
as the maximal spectra of the Hopf algebras

Z_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
and
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
, respectively.
We shall also need the opposite Borel
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}
and its central Hopf subalgebra

Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}
with maximal spectrum
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}
and correspondingly
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}
,

Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}
and
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}
.

1.3.
Main results

As said, this paper deals with the geometry of the Poisson algebraic group
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
towards understanding
the representation theory of large quantum groups.
This last question contain the description of the irreducible representations of contragredient quantum supergroups at roots of unity,
an important problem which is wide open even in the simplest case of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n))
.
We present a foundation for a thorough investigation of these representations.
We first summarize the main results in the following statement. Define the
reductive Lie algebra

\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{q}}:=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}\oplus\mathbb{C},&\mbox{if $\mathfrak{q}$ is of type super $A$}\\
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}},&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Type super
A
has the peculiarity that the rank of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is one less than the rank of Nichols algebra
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
. Hence we need to to enlarge the central subalgebra originally defined in
[
9
]
adding an extra group-like element in order to have a central subalgebra
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}
such that
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
is module-finite over
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

Our first main result is the following:

Theorem A
.

Let
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
be a large quantum group as above. Then

(a)

The pair
(U_{\mathfrak{q}},Z_{\mathfrak{q}})
has the structure of a Poisson order in the sense of
[
13
]
.

(b)

The algebraic Poisson group
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
is
solvable
.
The Lie bialgebra of
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
is dual to a Lie bialgebra structure on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{q}}

coming from an empty Belavin–Drinfeld triple; the symplectic leaves of
M_{\mathfrak{q}}

can be classified and related to conjugacy classes of the adjoint Lie group of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

(c)

Every
z\in M_{\mathfrak{q}}
with corresponding maximal ideal
\mathfrak{M}_{z}
gives rise to a finite-dimensional algebra

\mathcal{H}_{z}=U_{\mathfrak{q}}/U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{M}_{z}
. Then
\mathcal{H}_{z}\simeq\mathcal{H}_{z^{\prime}}
whenever

z
and
z^{\prime}
belong to the same symplectic leaf
S
. By abuse of notation we set
\mathcal{H}_{S}=\mathcal{H}_{z}
.
Every irreducible representation of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
is finite-dimensional and

\displaystyle\operatorname{Irr}U_{\mathfrak{q}}=\bigcup_{S\text{ symplectic leaf of }M}\operatorname{Irr}\mathcal{H}_{S}.

Furthermore, we have analogous results for the pairs
(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant},Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant})
and
(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+},Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+})
.
Next we make more precise the claims of Theorem
A
.
We fix a large quantum group
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

1.3.1.
Poisson orders

We denote by
\mathcal{Z}(A)
the center of an algebra
A
.
Because of the assumption that
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is deformable in the class of Nichols algebras as mentioned above,
we get Poisson order structures on the pairs
(U_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathcal{Z}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}))
,
(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant},\mathcal{Z}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}))
and

(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+},\mathcal{Z}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}))
by specialization. As these centers are singular, it is more convenient to look at the
central subalgebras in (
1.1
). Part
(a)
of Theorem
A

is included in the following result, see Theorem
6.2
.

Theorem B
.

The pairs
(U_{\mathfrak{q}},Z_{\mathfrak{q}})
,
(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant},Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant})
and
(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+},Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+})

have Poisson order structures in the sense of
[
13
]
obtained from specialization.

Presently it is not known whether for the remaining braiding matrices
\mathfrak{q}
in the list of
[
30
]

the pair
(U_{\mathfrak{q}},Z_{\mathfrak{q}})
has the structure of a Poisson order.
Indeed the other Nichols algebras of diagonal type with arithmetic root system
in the classification given in
[
30
]
do not admit such a one-parameter family
and for instance our proof of Theorem
6.2
does not generalize to them.

1.3.2.
Poisson algebraic groups and Lie bialgebras

Let
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{q}}
be a Cartan subalgebra of the semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}
and extend it to a Cartan subalgebra

\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
of the reductive Lie algebra
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
. We consider
a Lie bialgebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}
that corresponds to the empty Belavin–Drinfeld triple
[
11
]

and is explicitly defined in Theorem
7.8
and let

\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{q}}
be the Lie bialgebra dual to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{q}}\oplus\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
, cf. Eq. (
7.11
).

Let
G_{\mathfrak{q}}
be the semisimple algebraic group of adjoint type with
\operatorname{Lie}G_{\mathfrak{q}}\simeq\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}
. Denote

\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}:=\begin{cases}G_{\mathfrak{q}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times},&\mbox{if $\mathfrak{q}$ is of type super $A$}\\
G_{\mathfrak{q}},&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For instance, when
U_{\mathfrak{q}}=U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n))
,

\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}\simeq{\operatorname{PSL}}_{m}(\mathbb{C})\times{\operatorname{PSL}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
Let
\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\pm}
be a pair of opposite Borel subgroups of
\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}=\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}\cap\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}
be the
corresponding maximal torus and
N_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\pm}
be the unipotent radicals of
\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
; we identify
N_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}\simeq\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}/\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

Here is a more precise statement of Theorem
A
Part
(b)
,
see Theorems
7.10
and
8.2
.

Theorem C
.

(a)
The Poisson algebraic group
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
is isomorphic
to the product of two Borel subgroups of
\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}

and
\operatorname{Lie}M_{\mathfrak{q}}\simeq\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{q}}
as Lie bialgebras.

(b)
The symplectic leaves of
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
are in bijective correspondence
with the conjugacy classes of
\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}\times(\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}/\exp(\widetilde{Q}_{\mathfrak{q}}))
, where
\widetilde{Q}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a lattice
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
; each leaf is isomorphic to an open
dense subset of the corresponding conjugacy class.

Note that the symplectic leaves are not algebraic varieties in general.
The lattice
\widetilde{Q}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is related to the continuous parameter of the corresponding Lie bialgebra, see Appendix
B
.
In the case considered in
[
20
]
the Poisson structure is the standard one and
\widetilde{Q}_{\mathfrak{q}}
coincides
with the kernel of the exponential map restricted to the Cartan subalgebra.

Here are the promised versions for
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
and
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
.

Theorem D
.

(a)
The Poisson algebraic group
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
is isomorphic
to the Borel subgroup
\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
.
The Poisson structure is invariant under the left and right actions of
\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

(b)
The torus orbits of symplectic leaves of
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
are
the double Bruhat cells of
\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}
that lie in
\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
.

(c)
The algebraic group
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
is isomorphic
to the unipotent radical
N_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
of
\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
.
It has a Poisson structure arising from the identification
N_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}\simeq\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}/\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}
which
is invariant under the left action of
\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}

and is a reduction of the Poisson structure on
\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
from (a)
under the right action of
\widetilde{T}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

(d)
The torus orbits of symplectic leaves of
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
are
the open Richardson varieties of the flag variety
\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}/\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
that lie
inside an open Schubert cell identified with
\widetilde{N}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
.

See Theorems
8.4
and
8.7
. We refer to
[
23
,
37
]

for information on double Bruhat cells and open Richardson varieties.
Here we recall briefly the definitions.

Let
v,w\in W
.
The corresponding double Bruhat cell
is
\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{v,w}:=\widetilde{B}^{+}_{\mathfrak{q}}v\widetilde{B}^{+}_{\mathfrak{q}}\cap\widetilde{B}^{-}_{\mathfrak{q}}w\widetilde{B}^{-}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.
These cells form a partition:

\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}=\bigsqcup_{v,w\in W}\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{v,w}
.

In turn the corresponding open Richardson variety is

\mathring{X}^{v}_{w}=\mathring{X}^{v}\cap\mathring{X}_{w}\subset\widetilde{G}_{\mathfrak{q}}/\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}

where
\mathring{X}_{w}=\widetilde{B}^{-}_{\mathfrak{q}}w/\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
and
\mathring{X}^{v}=\widetilde{B}^{+}_{\mathfrak{q}}v/\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
are the Schubert cell and the opposite Schubert cell corresponding to
w
and
v
respectively.

1.3.3.
Representations

Since
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a free
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}
-module of finite rank, it
is a PI-algebra. Let
V
be an irreducible representation of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
;
by the preceding
V
is finite-dimensional and by the Schur Lemma,
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}
acts on
V
by
some
z\in M_{\mathfrak{q}}
(a central character) with corresponding maximal ideal
\mathfrak{M}_{z}
.
Now the algebra
\mathcal{H}_{z}=U_{\mathfrak{q}}/U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{M}_{z}
is non-zero and finite-dimensional and

V
becomes a
\mathcal{H}_{z}
-module. In other words the irreps of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
with central character
z

are in bijective correspondence with the irreps of
\mathcal{H}_{z}
. Thus

\displaystyle\operatorname{Irr}U_{\mathfrak{q}}=\bigsqcup_{z\in M_{\mathfrak{q}}}\operatorname{Irr}\mathcal{H}_{z}.

This circle of ideas is already present in
[
20
]
.
In this way, Part
(c)

of Theorem
A
boils down to the following statement.

Theorem E
.

For every two points
z,z^{\prime}
in the same symplectic leaf of

M_{\mathfrak{q}}
, the algebras
\mathcal{H}_{z}
and
\mathcal{H}_{z^{\prime}}
are isomorphic. In particular
there is a dimension preserving bijection between the
irreps of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
with central characters
z
and
z^{\prime}
.

See Theorem
8.2
.
For instance, let
z=e
be the identity of
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
. Then its symplectic leaf is
S=\{e\}

and
\mathcal{H}_{S}=\mathcal{H}_{e}
is the Drinfeld double of a suitable bosonization of the Nichols algebra
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Assume that the matrix
\mathfrak{q}
is of Cartan type. Then
\mathcal{H}_{e}
is a variation of
the small quantum group of Lusztig (with an extra copy of the finite torus),
with a notoriously difficult representation theory treated intensively in the literature.
Also, arguing as in
[
20
]
one concludes that
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a maximal order. It is a domain, hence a prime algebra.
It follows that for generic
z
,
\mathcal{H}_{z}
is semisimple by
[
14
, Theorem III.1.7]
.
But for super and modular types, the representation theory of
\mathcal{H}_{e}
is largely unknown,
except for the somewhat standard fact that simple modules are classified by highest weights
(but there is not even a conjecture for their characters). Also,
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
is not a maximal order because
it has nilpotent elements.

We next write down the corresponding formulations for
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
,
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}
,
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
and
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}
.
Let
\star\in\{\geqslant,\leqslant,+,-\}
. If
z\in M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\star}
, then we denote by

\mathfrak{M}_{z}^{\star}
its maximal ideal in
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\star}
and

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\star}

\displaystyle=U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\star}/U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\star}\left(\mathfrak{M}_{z}^{\star}\right).

Clearly these are finite-dimensional algebras.

Theorem F
.

(a)
For every
z,z^{\prime}
in the same double Bruhat cell inside
\widetilde{B}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
,
the algebras
\mathcal{H}^{\geqslant}_{z}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\geqslant}_{z^{\prime}}
are isomorphic.
Analogously for
\mathcal{H}^{\leqslant}_{z}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\leqslant}_{z^{\prime}}
.

(b)
For every
z,z^{\prime}
in the same open Richardson variety,
the algebras
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{z}
and
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{z^{\prime}}
are isomorphic.
Analogously for
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{z}
and
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{z^{\prime}}
.

See Theorems
8.4
and
8.7
.

Notice also that
\mathcal{H}_{z}
is a Hopf-Galois
\mathcal{H}_{e}
-object since
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a
cleft
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
-comodule algebra, see §
3.1
.
Analogously,

\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\star}
is a Hopf-Galois
\mathcal{H}_{e}^{\star}
-object for
\star\in\{\geqslant,\leqslant\}
.

1.4.
Strategy and organization

Our proofs of Theorems
C
–
F
follow a different strategy from that of

[
17
,
18
,
20
]
. These papers
rely on direct computations of Poisson brackets in terms of coordinates coming from Cartesian products of one-parameter unipotent
groups and subsequent reductions to the rank 2 case. This approach does not work in
the more general context of §
1.2
for several reasons,
the simplest of which is that the quantum Serre relations for quantum supergroups or for quantum groups at
-1

involve more than two Chevalley generators.

Instead our approach is based on intrinsic properties of pairings between restricted and
non-restricted integral forms of Hopf algebras.
It does not rely on reduction to low rank cases.
In particular, this approach provides new proofs of results in
[
17
,
18
,
20
]
.
We expect that these ideas could be applied to other situations not covered in this paper.

Next we overview briefly the main steps of the strategy:

Step 1
.

Let
\mathbb{C}(\nu)
be the field of rational functions on
\nu
and
\mathsf{A}
the subalgebra defined in (
5.1
).
Since
\mathfrak{q}
belongs to a family, there exists a chain of
\mathbb{C}(\nu)
-algebras

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+}\subset U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\geqslant}\subset U_{\mathbf{q}}

and non-restricted integral forms over
\mathsf{A}

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q},\mathsf{A}}^{+}\subset U_{\mathbf{q},\mathsf{A}}^{\geqslant}\subset U_{\mathbf{q},\mathsf{A}}

such that the algebras
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}\subset U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}\subset U_{\mathfrak{q}}
arise as specializations
from these integral forms. This provides Poisson order structures on the pairs
(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+},\mathcal{Z}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}))
,
(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant},\mathcal{Z}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}))

and
(U_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathcal{Z}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}))
.
This step is carried out in Section
5
in the framework of
[
20
,
13
]
evoked in Section
2
.

Step 2
.

We use Theorem
2.4
(on the restriction of Poisson order structures
obtained from specialization to central subalgebras) to prove that the Poisson order structures on

(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+},\mathcal{Z}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}))
and
(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant},\mathcal{Z}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}))
restrict to

(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+},Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+})
and
(U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant},Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant})
.
To get a Poisson order structure on
(U_{\mathfrak{q}},Z_{\mathfrak{q}})
by restriction from
(U_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathcal{Z}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}))
,
we need first to establish in Theorem
4.7

that the Weyl groupoid action preserves the central subalgebras
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Along the way we also obtain that these Poisson structures
on the algebras
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}
are equivariant under the Weyl groupoid. This step is carried out in Section
6
.

Step 3
.

This is the matter of Section
7

We introduce in §
5.4
non-restricted integral forms
U_{\mathbf{q},\mathsf{A}}^{\operatorname{res}\pm}
of
U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\pm}
and
\mathsf{A}
-linear perfect pairings

U_{\mathbf{q},\mathsf{A}}^{\operatorname{res}\pm}\times U_{\mathbf{q},\mathsf{A}}^{\mp}\to\mathsf{A}
. We prove

(i)

the specializations of
U_{\mathbf{q},\mathsf{A}}^{\operatorname{res}\pm}
are isomorphic to the Lusztig algebras
defined in
[
3
]
, see Proposition
5.9
;

(ii)

the cobrackets of the tangent Lie bialgebras to
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
and

M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}
are linearizations of those specializations, see Proposition
7.1
.

In this way we control tangent Lie bialgebras intrinsically and consequently
we compute in Theorems
7.4
and
7.8
the tangent Lie bialgebras of the
Poisson algebraic groups
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
and
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}
by means of a Manin pair.
Since these algebraic groups are connected we
describe them as Poisson algebraic groups in terms of Borel subgroups of complex semisimple algebraic groups of adjoint type.
Also,
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\pm}
are presented as Poisson homogeneous spaces.

Finally, we discuss in Section
8

the Poisson geometry of the Poisson algebraic groups
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
and the Poisson homogeneous
space
M_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
, and the applications to the irreducible representations of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
and
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
.

Besides, we discuss in Section
2
Poisson orders and their restrictions
to central subalgebras, see Theorem
2.4
;
Section
3
is devoted to preliminaries on Hopf algebra theory
while we present the main actors of this paper in Section
4
.

An in depth study of the restricted and nonrestricted integral forms of multiparameter
quantum groups of Cartan type at roots of unity was carried out in
[
26
]
, based on the interpretation
of those algebras as cocycle twists of the standard ones
[
17
,
18
]
.
The authors completely describe the specialization at
1
as the Poisson algebra of regular functions
on an explicit Poisson algebraic group and construct a Frobenius map which amounts to
a Hopf algebra isomorphism between the specialization at
1
and a central subalgebra
of the specialization at a root of unity. Our results show that this is an isomorphism of Poisson algebras
and that in the root of unity case it gives rise to a Poisson order structure.

Notations

The base field is
\mathbb{C}
; all algebras, Hom’s and tensor products are over
\mathbb{C}
.
If
t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t<n
, then
\mathbb{I}_{t,n}:=\{t,t+1,\dots,n\}
,

\mathbb{I}_{n}:=\mathbb{I}_{1,n}
.

For each integer
N>1
, let
\mathbb{G}_{N}
be
the group of
N
-th roots of unity in
\mathbb{C}
and let
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}_{N}
be its subset of primitive roots (of order
N
).
Also
\mathbb{G}_{\infty}=\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{G}_{N}
,
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}_{\infty}=\mathbb{G}_{\infty}-\{1\}
.

2.
Poisson orders and restrictions to central subalgebras

This section contains background on Poisson orders, their construction from specializations, and their relations to
Hopf algebras. We prove a general result on restrictions of Poisson orders to central subalgebras, Theorem
2.4
, which plays a key role later.

2.1.
Poisson orders

Here we follow the exposition in
[
20
, Chapter 3, §11]
.
Consider

\circ

a commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
A
and
h\in A
such that
A/h\simeq\mathbb{C}
,

\circ

an
A
-algebra
U
such that
h
is not a zero divisor of
U
. The natural map
U\to U/(h)
is denoted by
x\mapsto\overline{x}
.

For any
u\in U
such that
\overline{u}\in\mathcal{Z}(U/(h))
there is a linear map
D_{u}\in\operatorname{Hom}U/(h)
given by

(2.1)

\displaystyle D_{u}(y)

\displaystyle=\overline{\frac{[u,v]}{h}},

\displaystyle\text{if }y

\displaystyle=\overline{v}.

Proposition 2.1
.

[
20
, 11.7]
Let
u\in U
such that
\overline{u}\in\mathcal{Z}(U/(h))
.

(a)

D_{u}\in\operatorname{Der}U/(h)
.

(b)

Let
w\in U
. If
u^{\prime}=u+hw
so that
\overline{u}=\overline{u^{\prime}}
, then
D_{u}-D_{u^{\prime}}=\operatorname{ad}\overline{w}
is an inner derivation.
Conversely the inner derivation
\operatorname{ad}\overline{w}
coincides with
D_{hw}
.

(c)

Let
\varphi\in\operatorname{Aut}_{A-\rm{alg}}(U)
and let
\overline{\varphi}
be the induced automorphism of
U/(h)
. Then

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}\circ D_{u}\circ\overline{\varphi}^{\,-1}

\displaystyle=D_{\varphi(u)}.

(d)

There is natural Poisson structure on
\mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{Z}(U/(h))
given by

(2.2)

\displaystyle\{x,y\}=D_{u}(y)

\displaystyle=\overline{\frac{[u,v]}{h}},

\displaystyle\text{if }x

\displaystyle=\overline{u},y=\overline{v}.

(e)

The map
\varphi\mapsto\overline{\varphi}
gives a group homomorphism
\operatorname{Aut}_{A-\rm{alg}}(U)\to\operatorname{Aut}_{\rm{Poisson}}(\mathcal{Z})
.

(f)

\mathcal{L}=\{D_{v}:v\in U,\overline{v}\in\mathcal{Z}\}

is a Lie subalgebra of
\operatorname{Der}U/(h)
. Indeed

\displaystyle[D_{u},D_{v}]

\displaystyle=D_{\frac{[u,v]}{h}},

\displaystyle v\in U,\,\overline{v}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{Z}.

(g)

The Poisson structure gives rise to a Lie subalgebra
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
of
\operatorname{Der}\mathcal{Z}
that fits into the complex

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Innder}(U/(h))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

The sequence (
(g)
) is exact if and only if the Poisson center of
\mathcal{Z}
is trivial (i.e., there are no
Casimir elements
except 0). ∎

Brown and Gordon
[
13
]
axiomatized the ingredients of the above setting as follows:

Definition 2.2
.

A pair of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras
(R,Z)
is called a
Poisson order
if
Z
is a central subalgebra of
R
,
R
is a finitely generated
Z
-module and the following conditions hold:

(a)

Z
is equipped a structure of Poisson algebra
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
;

(b)

There exists a linear map
D:Z\to\operatorname{Der}_{\mathbb{C}}(R)
such that
D_{z}|_{Z}=\{z,-\}
for all
z\in Z
.

Reshetikhin, Voronov and Weinstein defined earlier a related notion of a
Poisson fibered algebra
, see
[
44
, Definition 2.1]
.
In the above terminology, such an algebra is a Poisson order with the additional property that

D_{z_{1}z_{2}}(r)=z_{1}D_{z_{2}}(r)+z_{2}D_{z_{1}}(r)\quad\mbox{for all}\;\;z_{1},z_{2}\in Z,r\in R.

Proposition
2.1
proves that the pair
(U/(h),\mathcal{Z}(U/(h)))
has a canonical
structure of Poisson order when
U/(h)
is module finite over
\mathcal{Z}(U/(h))
. The Poisson bracket on
\mathcal{Z}(U/(h))
is given by (
2.2
).
The linear map
D
is the map induced from the one in (
2.1
) by taking a linear section
of the canonical projection
U\to U/(h)
.

The main application of Poisson orders for us is the following result, inspired by
[
20
, Cor. 11.8]
,
[
19
, Cor. 9.2]
.
Assume that
R
is affine, i.e., it is a finitely generated algebra (hence also
Z
is affine).

Theorem 2.3
.

[
13
, Theorem 4.1]

Let
(R,Z)
be a Poisson order and

M:=\operatorname{MaxSpec}Z
.
Given
x\in M
with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{M}_{x}
, let
\mathcal{A}_{x}\coloneqq R/\mathfrak{M}_{x}R
, a finite dimensional algebra.
If
x
and
y
belong to the same symplectic core, then
\mathcal{A}_{x}\simeq\mathcal{A}_{y}
as algebras.

2.2.
Restrictions of Poisson orders from specializations

In the setting of Proposition
2.1
the center
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Z}(U/(h))
can be singular and is more useful to work with suitable subalgebras
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
.
Next we prove a general fact for the construction of Poisson orders on pairs
(U/(h),\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})
for subalgebras
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
defined from algebra automorphisms and skew-derivations. For this purpose we fix:

•

A
-algebra endomorphisms
\varsigma_{i}:U\to U
,
i\in\mathbb{I}
. We denote by
\overline{\varsigma}_{i}
the corresponding
\mathbb{C}
-algebra endomorphisms of
U/(h)
induced by
\varsigma_{i}
.

•

A
-linear
(\operatorname{id},\varsigma_{i})
-derivations

\partial_{i}:U\to U
,
i\in\mathbb{I}
. We denote by
\overline{\partial}_{i}
the corresponding
\mathbb{C}
-linear
(\operatorname{id},\overline{\varsigma}_{i})
-derivations induced by
\partial_{i}
.

Theorem 2.4
.

The Poisson order structure on
(U/(h),\mathcal{Z}(U/(h)))
from Proposition
2.1

restricts to a Poisson order structure on
(U/(h),\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})
, where

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{Z}\cap\left(\cap_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\ker\overline{\partial}_{i}\right)\cap\left(\cap_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\ker(\overline{\varsigma}_{i}-\operatorname{id})\right).

Proof.

We have to check that
\{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\}\subset\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
. Let
x_{j}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
and
u_{j}\in U
such that
x_{j}=\overline{u_{j}}
,
j=1,2
.
Fix
i\in\mathbb{I}
. As
\overline{\varsigma}_{i}(x_{j})=x_{j}
and
\partial_{i}(x_{i})=0
, there are
v_{j},w_{j}\in U
such that

\displaystyle\varsigma_{i}(u_{j})

\displaystyle=u_{j}+h\,v_{j},

\displaystyle\partial_{i}(u_{j})

\displaystyle=h\,w_{j},

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle=1,2.

Now we compute

\displaystyle\overline{\varsigma}_{i}\{x_{1},x_{2}\}

\displaystyle=\overline{\varsigma}_{i}\left(\overline{\frac{[u_{1},u_{2}]}{h}}\right)=\overline{\frac{[\varsigma_{i}(u_{1}),\varsigma_{i}(u_{2})]}{h}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\frac{[u_{1},u_{2}]}{h}}+\overline{[u_{1},v_{2}]}+\overline{[v_{1},u_{2}]}=\{x_{1},x_{2}\}+[x_{1},\overline{v_{2}}]+[\overline{v_{1}},x_{2}]=\{x_{1},x_{2}\},

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{i}\{x_{1},x_{2}\}

\displaystyle=\overline{\partial}_{i}\left(\overline{\frac{[u_{1},u_{2}]}{h}}\right)=\overline{\frac{\partial_{i}(u_{1})\varsigma_{i}(u_{2})+u_{1}\partial_{i}(u_{2})-\partial_{i}(u_{2})\varsigma_{i}(u_{1})-u_{2}\partial_{i}(u_{1})}{h}}

\displaystyle=\overline{w_{1}(u_{2}+h\,v_{2})+u_{1}w_{2}-w_{2}(u_{1}+h\,v_{1})-u_{2}w_{1}}=[x_{1},\overline{w_{2}}]+[\overline{w_{1}},x_{2}]=0.

Hence
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\in\ker\overline{\partial}_{i}\cap\ker(\overline{\varsigma}_{i}-\operatorname{id})
for all
i\in\mathbb{I}
so
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\in\mathcal{Z}
.
∎

2.3.
Poisson-Hopf algebras

Assume that in the above setting
U
is a Hopf algebra over
A
.
Then
U/(h)
has a canonical structure of Hopf algebra over
\mathbb{C}
.

Let
u\in U
such that
\overline{u}\in\mathcal{Z}(U/(h))
and furthermore
\Delta(\overline{u})\in\mathcal{Z}\left(U/(h)\otimes U/(h)\right)
.
Then

(2.6)

\displaystyle D_{\Delta(u)}\Delta(y)

\displaystyle=\Delta(D_{u}(y)),

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\in U/(h).

Proposition 2.5
.

[
20
, 11.7]
Let
B
be a central Hopf subalgebra of
U/(h)
.Then

\displaystyle T:=\text{ minimal subalgebra of $\mathcal{Z}$ containing $B$ and closed under the Poisson bracket}

is a central Hopf subalgebra of
U/(h)
, hence a Poisson-Hopf algebra.

We recall the elegant proof of
[
20
]
.

Proof.

Apply (
2.6
) to
y\in\mathcal{Z}
and
x=\overline{u}
to get
\Delta(\{x,y\})=\{\Delta(x),\Delta(y)\}
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{Z}
.
Hence
\widetilde{T}=\{t\in T:\Delta(t)\in T\otimes T\}
, which is a subalgebra containing
B
, is also closed under Poisson bracket; thus
\widetilde{T}=T
.
∎

3.
Hopf algebras

In this section we collect preliminaries on (braided) Hopf algebras (always with bijective antipode
\mathcal{S}
), bosonizations,
braided vectors spaces of diagonal type, Nichols algebras, Weyl groupoids, distinguished
pre-Nichols algebras and Lusztig algebras. We refer to
[
42
,
1
]
for more information on Hopf algebras, Nichols algebras, Nichols algebras of diagonal type, respectively.

3.1.
Cleft comodule algebras

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hopf algebra with a central Hopf subalgebra
Z
.
Given
z\in G=\operatorname{Alg}(Z,\mathbb{C})
(the pro-algebraic group defined by
Z
), let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{z}

\displaystyle=\ker z,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{z}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}\mathfrak{M}_{z},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{z}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}/\mathfrak{I}_{z};

thus
\mathcal{H}_{z}
is an algebra (with multiplication
m_{z}
and unit
u_{z}
)
and the natural projection
p_{z}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{z}
is an algebra map.
Then
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
is a quotient Hopf algebra of
\mathcal{H}
and
there is an exact sequence of Hopf algebras
Z\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
.
Also for any
z,z^{\prime}\in G
there are well-defined algebra morphisms

\Delta_{z,z^{\prime}}:\mathcal{H}_{zz^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{H}_{z}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{z}^{\prime}
and in particular the maps

\displaystyle\varrho_{z}

\displaystyle:=\Delta_{z,\varepsilon}:\mathcal{H}_{z}\to\mathcal{H}_{z}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\lambda_{z}:=\Delta_{\varepsilon,z}:\mathcal{H}_{z}\to\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{z},

make
\mathcal{H}_{z}
a
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
-bicomodule algebra for
z\in G
.
Clearly

(3.1)

\displaystyle\varrho_{z}p_{z}

\displaystyle=(p_{z}\otimes p_{\varepsilon})\Delta_{\mathcal{H}},

\displaystyle\lambda_{z}p_{z}=(p_{\varepsilon}\otimes p_{z})\Delta_{\mathcal{H}}.

Recall that a right
K
-comodule algebra
A
(over a Hopf algebra
K
)
is cleft if there exists a convolution-invertible morphism of
K
-comodules

\chi:K\to A
.

Lemma 3.1
.

If the
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
-comodule algebra
\mathcal{H}
with coaction
\varrho=(\operatorname{id}\otimes p_{\varepsilon})\Delta_{\mathcal{H}}

is cleft, then so is
\mathcal{H}_{z}
for any
z\in G
. In particular
\mathcal{H}_{z}
is a Hopf-Galois object over
\mathcal{H}_{e}
.

If
\mathcal{H}
is a pointed Hopf algebra, then
\mathcal{H}_{z}
is
\mathcal{H}_{e}
-cleft for all
z\in G
.

Proof.

If
\chi:\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{H}
is a morphism of
\mathcal{H}
-comodules, then so is

\chi_{z}:=p_{z}\chi:\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{H}_{z}
:

\displaystyle(\chi_{z}\otimes\operatorname{id})\varrho_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=(p_{z}\otimes\operatorname{id})(\chi\otimes\operatorname{id})\Delta_{\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=(p_{z}\otimes\operatorname{id})(\operatorname{id}\otimes p_{\varepsilon})\Delta_{\mathcal{H}}\chi=\varrho_{z}p_{z}\chi=\varrho_{z}\chi_{z}.

If
\chi
is convolution-invertible, then so is
\chi_{z}

since
p_{z}
is an algebra map.

For the last statement,
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
-cleft by
[
46
, 4.3]
, and then we apply the first part.
∎

We refer to
[
45
]
for Hopf-Galois objects.
In the setting of Cayley–Hamilton Hopf algebras, which is a refinement of the above setting for the pair
(\mathcal{H},Z)
,
a tensor product decomposition of the
irreducible representations of
\mathcal{H}_{z}
was obtained in
[
21
]
.

3.2.
Braided Hopf algebras and bosonization

Recall that a braided vector space is a pair
({\mathcal{V}},c)
where
{\mathcal{V}}
is a vector space
and
c\in GL({\mathcal{V}}\otimes{\mathcal{V}})
is a solution of the braid equation:
(c\otimes\operatorname{id})(\operatorname{id}\otimes c)(c\otimes\operatorname{id})=(\operatorname{id}\otimes c)(c\otimes\operatorname{id})(\operatorname{id}\otimes c)
. There are natural notions of morphisms of braided vector spaces and braided Hopf algebras (braided vector spaces with compatible algebra and coalgebra structures), see
[
48
]
for details. To distinguish comultiplications of braided Hopf algebras from those of Hopf algebras,
we use a variation of the Sweedler notation for the former:
\underline{\Delta}(r)=r^{(1)}\otimes r^{(2)}
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra. Then the category of (left) Yetter-Drinfeld modules
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
is
a braided tensor category and there is a forgetful functor from
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
to the category of braided vector spaces, namely

{\mathcal{V}}\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
goes to
({\mathcal{V}},c)
where
c\in GL({\mathcal{V}}\otimes{\mathcal{V}})
is given by
c(v\otimes w)=v_{(-1)}\cdot w\otimes v_{(0)}
in Sweedler notation. This forgetful functor sends Hopf algebras in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
to braided Hopf algebras. In turn Hopf algebras in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
are noteworthy because of the Radford-Majid bosonization that provides a bijective correspondence between their collection and the collection of triples
(A,\pi,\iota)
where

A\overset{\pi}{\underset{\iota}{\rightleftarrows}}H
are morphisms of Hopf algebras with
\pi\iota=\operatorname{id}_{H}
. See
[
42
]
for an exposition. More precisely, the correspondence sends the Hopf algebra
R\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
to the bosonization
R\#H
and the triple
(A,\pi,\iota)
to
the algebra of right coinvariants
R=A^{\operatorname{co}\pi}
.

Similar notions and results hold for the category of (right) Yetter-Drinfeld
modules
\mathcal{YD}^{H}_{H}
consisting of right
H
-modules and right
H
-comodules
{\mathcal{V}}
satisfying the compatibility

\displaystyle(v\cdot h)_{(0)}\otimes(v\cdot h)_{(1)}

\displaystyle=v_{(0)}\cdot h_{(2)}\otimes\mathcal{S}(h_{(1)})v_{(1)}h_{(3)},

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{V}},\;h\in H.

For convenience of the reader we spell out the precise definitions. First, any
{\mathcal{V}}\in\mathcal{YD}^{H}_{H}
becomes a braided vector space
with
c\in GL({\mathcal{V}}\otimes{\mathcal{V}})
and its inverse given by

(3.2)

\displaystyle c(v\otimes w)

\displaystyle=w_{(0)}\otimes v\cdot w_{(1)},

\displaystyle c^{-1}(v\otimes w)

\displaystyle=w\cdot\mathcal{S}^{-1}(v_{(1)})\otimes v_{(0)},

\displaystyle v,w

\displaystyle\in V.

Let
(A,\pi,\iota)
be a triple as before. Then the subalgebra of left coinvariants

\displaystyle S={}^{\operatorname{co}\pi}A=\{s\in A:(\pi\otimes\operatorname{id})\Delta(s)=1\otimes s\}

becomes a Hopf algebra in
\mathcal{YD}^{H}_{H}
with right action
\cdot
, right
coaction
\rho
and comultiplication
\underline{\Delta}
given by

\displaystyle s\cdot h

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}(h_{(1)})sh_{(2)},

\displaystyle\rho(s)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}\otimes\pi)\Delta(s),

\displaystyle\underline{\Delta}(s)

\displaystyle=s_{(1)}\otimes\underline{\vartheta}(s_{(2)}),

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\in S,h\in H,

where
\underline{\vartheta}:A\to S
is given by
\underline{\vartheta}(a)=\pi(\mathcal{S}(a_{(1)}))a_{(2)}
,
a\in A
.
Conversely, the bosonization
H\#S
of a Hopf algebra
S
in
\mathcal{YD}^{H}_{H}
is the vector space

H\otimes S
with the right smash product and coproduct. That is, given
s,\widetilde{s}\in S
and

h,\widetilde{h}\in H
,

\displaystyle(h\#s)(\widetilde{h}\#\widetilde{s})

\displaystyle=h\widetilde{h}_{(1)}\#(s\cdot\widetilde{h}_{(2)})\widetilde{s},

\displaystyle\Delta(h\#s)

\displaystyle=h_{(1)}\#(s^{(1)})_{(0)}\otimes h_{(2)}(s^{(1)})_{(1)}\#s^{(2)}.

3.3.
Nichols algebras

Let
{\mathcal{V}}\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
. Then the tensor algebra
T({\mathcal{V}})
is naturally a Hopf algebra in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
.
A
pre-Nichols algebra
of
{\mathcal{V}}
is a factor of
T({\mathcal{V}})

by a graded Hopf ideal in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
supported in degrees
\geqslant 2
.
The maximal Hopf ideal among those is denoted by
{\mathcal{J}}({\mathcal{V}})
;
the Nichols algebra of
{\mathcal{V}}
is the quotient
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{V}})=T({\mathcal{V}})/{\mathcal{J}}({\mathcal{V}})
.

The tensor algebra of a braided vector space
({\mathcal{V}},c)
is also a braided Hopf algebra in the sense of
[
48
]
;
a
pre-Nichols algebra
of
{\mathcal{V}}
is a factor of
T({\mathcal{V}})

by a braided graded Hopf ideal supported in degrees
\geqslant 2
.
The maximal Hopf ideal among those is denoted
\widetilde{{\mathcal{J}}}({\mathcal{V}})
;
the Nichols algebra of
{\mathcal{V}}
is the quotient
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{V}})=T({\mathcal{V}})/\widetilde{{\mathcal{J}}}({\mathcal{V}})
.

These two structures are compatible, i.e., if
{\mathcal{V}}\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
and
({\mathcal{V}},c)
is the corresponding
braided vector space, then
{\mathcal{J}}({\mathcal{V}})=\widetilde{{\mathcal{J}}}({\mathcal{V}})
. But a pre-Nichols algebra of

({\mathcal{V}},c)
does not necessarily come as the forgetful functor applied to a pre-Nichols algebra of

{\mathcal{V}}\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Let
H
be cosemisimple,
{\mathcal{V}}\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
and
G={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{V}})\#H=\oplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}G^{n}
, where
G^{n}={\mathcal{B}}^{n}({\mathcal{V}})\#H
.
By other characterizations of Nichols algebras, we know that

(a)

{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{V}})
is coradically graded and generated in degree 1;

(b)

G
is coradically graded and generated in degree 1.

Since the projection
\pi:G\to H
is graded, the subalgebra of left coinvariants

S={}^{\operatorname{co}\pi}G
inherits the grading of
G
; by a standard argument it is also coradically graded and generated in degree 1. Thus
S
is a Nichols algebra in
\mathcal{YD}^{H}_{H}
.

3.4.
Hopf skew-pairings of bosonizations

Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:M\times V\to\mathbb{C}
be a bilinear form
between two vector spaces
M
and
V
. We denote by

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:(M\otimes M)\times(V\otimes V)\to\mathbb{C}
the bilinear form
determined by

(3.3)

\displaystyle\langle m\otimes m^{\prime},v\otimes v^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle m,v^{\prime}\rangle\langle m^{\prime},v\rangle,

\displaystyle m,m^{\prime}

\displaystyle\in M,\;v,v^{\prime}\in V.

Let
H
and
K
be two Hopf algebras.
A bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:K\times H\to\mathbb{C}
is a
Hopf skew-pairing
(or skew-pairing of Hopf algebras) if for all for
k,k^{\prime}\in K
,
h,h^{\prime}\in H
,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle k,hh^{\prime}\rangle&=\langle\Delta^{\operatorname{op}}(k),h\otimes h^{\prime}\rangle,&\langle kk^{\prime},h\rangle&=\langle k\otimes k^{\prime},\Delta(h)\rangle,&&\\
\langle k,1\rangle&=\varepsilon(k),&\langle 1,h\rangle&=\varepsilon(h),&\langle\mathcal{S}(k),h\rangle&=\langle k,\mathcal{S}(h)\rangle.\end{aligned}

A skew-pairing of braided Hopf algebras is defined by (
3.4
) but with the convention

\displaystyle\underline{\Delta}^{\operatorname{op}}=c^{-1}\underline{\Delta}.

Let us fix a Hopf skew-pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:K\times H\to\mathbb{C}
.
A
YD-pairing
between
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{YD}^{K}_{K}
and
{\mathcal{V}}\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
is a bilinear form

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathcal{M}\times{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathbb{C}
such that

(3.5)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle m\cdot k,v\rangle&=\langle k,v_{(-1)}\rangle\langle m,v_{(0)}\rangle,&\\
\langle m,h\cdot v\rangle&=\langle m_{(1)},h\rangle\langle m_{(0)},v\rangle,\end{aligned}

\displaystyle m\in\mathcal{M},\;k

\displaystyle\in K,\;v\in{\mathcal{V}},\;h\in H.

We recall the following well-known result, whose proof is straightforward.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
R
be a Hopf algebra in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
,
S
be a Hopf algebra in
\mathcal{YD}^{K}_{K}

and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:(K\#S)\times(R\#H)\to\mathbb{C}
be a bilinear form such that

(3.6)

\displaystyle\langle ky,xh\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle k,h\rangle\langle y,x\rangle,

\displaystyle y\in S,\;k

\displaystyle\in K,\;x\in R,\;h\in H.

Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is a Hopf skew-pairing.

(b)

The restriction of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
to
K\times H
is a Hopf skew-pairing
and the restriction of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
to
S\times R
is both a skew-pairing of braided Hopf algebras and a YD-pairing.∎

A YD-pairing between
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{YD}^{K}_{K}
and
{\mathcal{V}}\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
extends canonically to a YD-pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:T(\mathcal{M})\times T({\mathcal{V}})\to\mathbb{C}
. This extension is actually
a braided Hopf skew-pairing, i.e., it satisfies (
3.4
) with respect to the braided
comultiplications. The bilinear form

\displaystyle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:

\displaystyle(K\#T(\mathcal{M}))\times(T({\mathcal{V}})\#H)\to\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle\langle k\#y,x\#h\rangle

\displaystyle:=\langle k,h\rangle\langle y,x\rangle,

y\in T(\mathcal{M})
,
k\in K
,
x\in T({\mathcal{V}})
,
h\in H
is a Hopf skew-pairing by Lemma
3.3
.

Assume that
\dim\mathcal{M}<\infty
. Then the radical
T(\mathcal{M}^{*})^{\perp}

with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
coincides with
{\mathcal{J}}(\mathcal{M})
. Hence, for any
{\mathcal{V}}
YD-paired with
\mathcal{M}
we have

\displaystyle T({\mathcal{V}})^{\perp}\supseteq{\mathcal{J}}(\mathcal{M}).

Consequently, if
\dim\mathcal{M}<\infty
and
\dim{\mathcal{V}}<\infty
,
{\mathcal{B}}
is a pre-Nichols algebra of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{YD}^{K}_{K}
and
\mathcal{E}
is a pre-Nichols algebra of
{\mathcal{V}}
in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
,
then
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle

descends to Hopf skew-pairings

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:{\mathcal{B}}\times\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{C}
and

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:(K\#{\mathcal{B}})\times(\mathcal{E}\#H)\to\mathbb{C}
.

3.5.
Nichols algebras of diagonal type

We fix
\theta\in\mathbb{N}
and set
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{I}_{\theta}
.
Let
(V,c)
be a (complex) braided vector space of diagonal type with braiding matrix

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}=(q_{ij})\in\big(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\big)^{\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}}

with respect to a basis
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
,
i.e.,
c(x_{i}\otimes x_{j})=q_{ij}x_{j}\otimes x_{i}
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{I}
.
We assume that
\dim{\mathcal{B}}(V)<\infty
. These Nichols algebras are classified in
[
30
]
.
Throughout the paper we will also assume that the Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{q}
is connected, for simplicity of the exposition.

The canonical basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}
is denoted
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\theta}
.
The algebra
T(V)
is
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}
-graded, with grading
\deg x_{i}=\alpha_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{I}
.
This grading naturally specializes to the standard
\mathbb{N}_{0}
-grading.

Let
\mathfrak{q}:\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}

be the
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinear forms associated to the matrix
\mathfrak{q}
, i.e.,

\mathfrak{q}(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}):=q_{jk}
,
j,k\in\mathbb{I}
.
If
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}
and
i\in\mathbb{I}
, then we set

(3.8)

\displaystyle q_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}(\alpha,\beta),

\displaystyle q_{\alpha\alpha}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}(\alpha,\alpha),

\displaystyle N_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{ord}q_{\alpha\alpha},

\displaystyle N_{i}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{ord}q_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i}}=N_{\alpha_{i}}.

Remark 3.4
.

Every
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}
-graded pre-Nichols algebra of
V
admits algebra automorphisms
\varsigma_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}

and
(\operatorname{id},\varsigma_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}})
-derivations
\partial_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}
for each
i\in\mathbb{I}
; that is,

\displaystyle\partial_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(xy)

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(x)\varsigma_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(y)+x\partial_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(y),

\displaystyle x,y\in T(V).

Indeed the algebra automorphism
\varsigma_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}:T(V)\to T(V)
is given by

\displaystyle\varsigma_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}(\alpha_{i},\beta)x,

\displaystyle x\in T(V)

\displaystyle\;\;\text{homogeneous of degree }\beta\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}.

The linear endomorphisms
\partial_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}:T(V)\to T(V)
are defined as follows.
Let
\Delta_{m,n}(x)
be the homogeneous component of
\Delta(x)\in T(V)\otimes T(V)
of degree
(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
.
Then

\displaystyle\Delta_{n-1,1}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\partial_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(x)\otimes x_{i},

\displaystyle x\in T^{n}(V).

It is easy to see that
\partial_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}
is a
(\operatorname{id},\varsigma_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}})
-derivation.
If
{\mathcal{B}}
is a quotient of
T(V)
by a
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}
-homogeneous ideal, then
\varsigma_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}
induces an algebra automorphism of
{\mathcal{B}}
, also denoted by
\varsigma_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}
, and
\partial_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}
induces a
(\operatorname{id},\varsigma_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}})
-derivation of
{\mathcal{B}}
, also denoted by
\partial_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}
.

3.6.
Weyl groupoids

The notions of Weyl groupoid and generalized root systems
were introduced in
[
29
,
32
]
. We recall the main features needed later.
Let
(c_{ij}^{\mathfrak{q}})_{i,j\in\mathbb{I}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}}
be the (generalized Cartan) matrix
defined by
c_{ii}^{\mathfrak{q}}:=2
and

(3.9)

\displaystyle c_{ij}^{\mathfrak{q}}

\displaystyle:=-\min\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:(n+1)_{q_{ii}}(1-q_{ii}^{n}q_{ij}q_{ji})=0\right\},

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\neq j.

Let
i\in\mathbb{I}
.
First, the reflection
s_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}\in GL(\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}})
is given by

(3.10)

\displaystyle s_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\alpha_{j})

\displaystyle:=\alpha_{j}-c_{ij}^{\mathfrak{q}}\alpha_{i},

\displaystyle j\in\mathbb{I}.

Second, the matrix
\rho_{i}(\mathfrak{q})
is given by

(3.11)

\displaystyle(\rho_{i}(\mathfrak{q}))_{jk}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{q}(s_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\alpha_{j}),s_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\alpha_{k}))=q_{jk}q_{ik}^{-c_{ij}^{\mathfrak{q}}}q_{ji}^{-c_{ik}^{\mathfrak{q}}}q_{ii}^{c_{ij}^{\mathfrak{q}}c_{ik}^{\mathfrak{q}}},

\displaystyle j,k\in\mathbb{I}.

Finally, the braided vector space
\rho_{i}(V)
is of diagonal type with matrix
\rho_{i}(\mathfrak{q})
. Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}:=\{\rho_{j_{1}}\dots\rho_{j_{n}}(\mathfrak{q}):j_{1},\dots,j_{n}\in\mathbb{I},n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

The set
\mathcal{X}
is called the Weyl-equivalence class of
\mathfrak{q}
.
The set
\varDelta_{+}^{\mathfrak{q}}
of
positive roots
consists of the
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}
-degrees of the generators of a PBW-basis of
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
, counted with multiplicities.
Let
\varDelta^{\mathfrak{q}}:=\varDelta_{+}^{\mathfrak{q}}\cup-\varDelta_{+}^{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Then the generalized root system of
\mathfrak{q}
is the fibration
\Delta\to\mathcal{X}
, where the fiber of
\rho_{j_{1}}\dots\rho_{j_{N}}(\mathfrak{q})
is
\Delta^{\rho_{j_{1}}\dots\rho_{j_{N}}(\mathfrak{q})}
. The Weyl groupoid
\mathcal{W}_{\mathfrak{q}}

of
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}

acts on this fibration, generalizing the classical Weyl group.
Here is another characterization of
\varDelta_{+}^{\mathfrak{q}}
, valid because it is finite.
Let
\omega_{0}^{\mathfrak{q}}\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathfrak{q}}
be an element of maximal length and
\omega_{0}^{\mathfrak{q}}=\sigma_{i_{1}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\sigma_{i_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{\ell}}

be a reduced expression. Then

(3.12)

\displaystyle\beta_{k}

\displaystyle:=s_{i_{1}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\cdots s_{i_{k-1}}(\alpha_{i_{k}}),

\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{I}_{\ell}

are pairwise different vectors and
\varDelta_{+}^{\mathfrak{q}}=\{\beta_{k}:k\in\mathbb{I}_{\ell}\}

[
16
, Prop. 2.12]
, so
|\varDelta_{+}^{\mathfrak{q}}|=\ell
.

3.7.
Cartan roots
[
9
]

This important notion is crucial for our purposes. First,
i\in\mathbb{I}
is a
Cartan vertex
of
\mathfrak{q}
if

(3.13)

\displaystyle q_{ij}q_{ji}

\displaystyle=q_{ii}^{c_{ij}^{\mathfrak{q}}},

\displaystyle\text{for all }j\neq i.

Then the set of
Cartan roots
of
\mathfrak{q}
is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{O}^{\mathfrak{q}}

\displaystyle=\{s_{i_{1}}^{\mathfrak{q}}s_{i_{2}}\dots s_{i_{k}}(\alpha_{i})\in\varDelta^{\mathfrak{q}}:i\in\mathbb{I}\text{ is a Cartan vertex of }\rho_{i_{k}}\dots\rho_{i_{2}}\rho_{i_{1}}(\mathfrak{q})\}.

Set
\mathfrak{O}^{\mathfrak{q}}_{+}=\mathfrak{O}^{\mathfrak{q}}\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\theta}
. Recall (
3.8
)
and set
\widetilde{N}_{\beta}:=N_{\beta}
, if
\beta\notin\mathfrak{O}^{\mathfrak{q}}
,
or else
\infty
if
\beta\in\mathfrak{O}^{\mathfrak{q}}
.

The set of Cartan roots gives rise to a root system up to a rescaling. Set

(3.14)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathfrak{O}}^{\mathfrak{q}}

\displaystyle=\{N^{\mathfrak{q}}_{\beta}\beta:\beta\in\mathfrak{O}^{\mathfrak{q}}\},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathfrak{O}}^{\mathfrak{q}}_{+}

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathfrak{O}}^{\mathfrak{q}}\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\theta},

\displaystyle\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=N^{\mathfrak{q}}_{\beta}\beta,\ \beta\in\mathfrak{O}^{\mathfrak{q}}.

Theorem 3.5
.

[
5
, Theorem 3.6]
The set
\underline{\mathfrak{O}}^{\mathfrak{q}}
is either empty or
a root system inside the real vector space generated by
\mathfrak{O}^{\mathfrak{q}}
.
The set
\varPi^{\mathfrak{q}}
of all indecomposable elements of
\underline{\mathfrak{O}}^{\mathfrak{q}}_{+}
is a basis of this root system. ∎

Here
\underline{\gamma}\in\underline{\mathfrak{O}}^{\mathfrak{q}}_{+}
is

indecomposable
if it can not be represented as a non-trivial positive linear combination of elements of
\underline{\mathfrak{O}}^{\mathfrak{q}}_{+}
.
Let
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}
be either
0
or the semisimple Lie algebra with root system as in Theorem
3.5
, accordingly.
We fix a triangular decomposition

(3.15)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{q}}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}

and the Borel subalgebras
\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\pm}=\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{q}}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\pm}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}
;
if
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}=0
, then
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}=\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{q}}=\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}=0
.
We denote the root lattice of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{q}}
by

(3.16)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathfrak{q}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\gamma\in\underline{\mathfrak{O}}^{\mathfrak{q}}_{+}}\mathbb{Z}\gamma=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\varPi^{\mathfrak{q}}}\mathbb{Z}\gamma.

3.8.
Distinguished pre-Nichols algebras

The finite-dimensional Nichols algebras of diagonal type admit distinguished pre-Nichols algebras introduced in
[
8
,
9
]
.
An ideal
{\mathcal{I}}(V)
of
T(V)
was introduced in
[
9
]
;
it is generated by all the defining relations of
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
in
[
8
, Theorem 3.1]
,
but excluding the power root vectors
x_{\alpha}^{N_{\alpha}}
,
\alpha\in\underline{\mathfrak{O}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
and adding some quantum Serre relations, see
[
9
]
for the precise list of relations.

Definition 3.6
.

[
9
]

The distinguished pre-Nichols algebra
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
of
V
is the quotient

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}=T(V)/{\mathcal{I}}(V)
. Since
{\mathcal{I}}(V)
is a Hopf ideal,
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a braided Hopf algebra.

By Remark
3.4
, there are automorphisms
\varsigma_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}

and skew-derivations
\partial_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
i\in\mathbb{I}
.

3.9.
Lusztig algebras

The
Lusztig algebra

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{q}}
associated to
\mathfrak{q}
is the graded dual of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}

[
3
]
.
Thus
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a braided Hopf algebra equipped with a bilinear form
(\,,\,):\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{q}}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
which satisfies

(3.17)

\displaystyle(y,xx^{\prime})=(y^{(2)},x)(y^{(1)},x^{\prime})\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad(yy^{\prime},x)=(y,x^{(2)})(y^{\prime},x^{(1)})

for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Let
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}=\,^{\operatorname{co}\varpi}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
be the subalgebra of coinvariants of the canonical projection

\varpi:\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}\to{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}.

Then
Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
is a
normal
Hopf subalgebra of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}

[
9
, Theorem 29]
and
we have an extension of braided Hopf algebras

Z_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}\overset{\iota}{\hookrightarrow}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}\overset{\varpi}{\twoheadrightarrow}{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Taking graded duals, we obtain a new extension of braided Hopf algebras, cf.
[
4
, Proposition 3.2]
:

(3.18)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}^{t}}\overset{\varpi^{*}}{\hookrightarrow}\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{q}}\overset{\iota^{*}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathfrak{q}},

Remark 3.7
.

Assume that (
4.26
) below holds. Then
the braided Hopf algebra
\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a Hopf algebra, isomorphic to the enveloping algebra
of the Lie algebra
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathfrak{q}})

[
4
, 3.3]
.
Moreover
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{Z}_{\mathfrak{q}})\simeq\mathfrak{n}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}
as in (
3.15
)
[
5
]
.

4.
Large quantum groups

In this section we describe the
large quantum groups
, i.e., Drinfeld doubles of
bosonizations of the distinguished pre-Nichols algebras belonging to a one-parameter family;
these are the main focus of the paper.
The large quantum Borel and unipotent subalgebras are also introduced here.
Throughout the rest of the paper
\Gamma^{+}
and
\Gamma^{-}
denote free abelian groups of rank
\theta
with bases denoted respectively
(K_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and

(L_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
. Let
\Gamma=\Gamma^{+}\times\Gamma^{-}
.

4.1.
Families of Nichols algebras

From now on we assume that
\mathfrak{q}
belongs to a one-parameter family (except when explicitly stated otherwise).
This means that there exists an indecomposable matrix

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}=(\mathbf{q}_{ij})\in\big(\mathbb{C}[\nu^{\pm 1}]^{\times}\big)^{\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}}

such that:

\circ

The Nichols algebra of the
\mathbb{C}(\nu)
-braided vector space of diagonal type
V_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}
with basis
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
and braiding matrix (
4.1
) has finite root system;
thus
\mathbf{q}
is in the list of
[
30
]
.

\circ

There exists an open subset
\emptyset\neq O\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that for any
x\in O
, the matrix
\mathbf{q}(x)
obtained by
evaluation
\nu\mapsto x
has the same finite root system as
\mathbf{q}
.

\circ

There exists
\xi\in\mathbb{G}_{\infty}^{\prime}\cap O
such that
\mathfrak{q}=\mathbf{q}(\xi)
.

By inspection in
[
30
]
, all one-parameter families are listed in the Appendix
A
.
We denote the Nichols algebras of
V
and
V_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}
, with braidings given by
\mathfrak{q}
, respectively
\mathbf{q}
, by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}

\displaystyle:={\mathcal{B}}(V)

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle:={\mathcal{B}}(V_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}).

The defining relations and PBW-basis of
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{q}}
are described in
[
2
]

over an algebraically closed field of characteristic 0 but the same presentation and PBW-basis are valid over
\mathbb{C}(\nu)
. Indeed, apply to
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}(\nu)
,
\mathbb{K}=\overline{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}
the following remarks:

\circ

Let
\mathbb{K}/\mathbb{F}
be a field extension and
(V,c)
a braided
\mathbb{F}
-vector space. Then
(V\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}\mathbb{K},c\otimes\operatorname{id})
is a braided
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
and
{\mathcal{B}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}\mathbb{K}\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(V\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}\mathbb{K})
; use e.g. quantum symmetrizers.

\circ

Let
\mathbb{K}/\mathbb{F}
be a faithfully flat extension of commutative rings.
Let
U
be a
\mathbb{F}
-algebra with generators
(y_{j})_{j\in J}
and
U_{\mathbb{K}}=U\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}\mathbb{K}
which is also generated by
(y_{j})_{j\in J}
.
Let
(r_{t})_{t\in T}
be a set of elements in the tensor algebra over
\mathbb{F}
of the free module
\mathbb{F}^{(J)}
. Then these are
defining relations of
U
if and only if they are defining relations of
U_{\mathbb{K}}
.

The discussions in §
3.5
and §
3.6
apply to the matrix
\mathbf{q}
.
Let
\mathbf{q}:\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}\to(\mathbb{C}[\nu^{\pm 1}])^{\times}
as in §
3.5
; we also have the notation

\mathbf{q}_{\alpha\beta}
for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{I}}
as in (
3.8
).
We denote by
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{q}}
the corresponding Weyl groupoid,
by
\rho_{i}(\mathfrak{q})
the related braiding matrices, etc.
As in Remark
3.4
, there are

\varsigma_{i}^{\mathbf{q}}\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\rm{alg}}({\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{q}})
and
(\operatorname{id},\varsigma_{i}^{\mathbf{q}})
-derivations

\partial_{i}^{\mathbf{q}}:{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{q}}\to{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{q}}
, for every
i\in\mathbb{I}
.

Remark 4.1
.

Let
\beta\in\Delta^{\mathbf{q}}
. Crucially,

\beta
is a Cartan root of
\mathfrak{q}
if and only if
\operatorname{ord}\mathbf{q}_{\beta\beta}=\infty
.

4.2.
The quantum group
U_{\mathbf{q}}

Here we work over
\mathbb{C}(\nu)
.
Let
W_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}
the
\mathbb{C}(\nu)
-vector space with basis
(y_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
.
The group
\Gamma
acts on
V_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}\oplus W_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}
by

(4.2)

\displaystyle K_{i}\cdot x_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{ij}x_{j},

\displaystyle K_{i}\cdot y_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{ij}^{-1}y_{j},

\displaystyle L_{i}\cdot x_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{ji}x_{j},

\displaystyle L_{i}\cdot y_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{ji}^{-1}y_{j},

i,j\in\mathbb{I}
. The vector space
V_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}\oplus W_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}
is
\Gamma
-graded by

(4.3)

\displaystyle\deg x_{i}

\displaystyle=K_{i},

\displaystyle\deg y_{i}

\displaystyle=L_{i},

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{I}.

Thus
V_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}\oplus W_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}\in{}^{\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma}_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma}\mathcal{YD}
with coaction given by the grading. In particular,

W_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}
is a braided vector space with braiding matrix
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
where

\mathbf{q}^{\prime}_{ij}=\mathbf{q}_{ji}^{-1}
,
i,j\in\mathbb{I}
.

We define
U_{\mathbf{q}}
as the quotient Hopf algebra
of the bosonization
T(V_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}\oplus W_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)})\#\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma
modulo the ideal
generated by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}(V_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}(W_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}),

\displaystyle x_{i}y_{j}-\mathbf{q}_{ij}^{-1}y_{j}x_{i}-\delta_{ij}(K_{i}L_{i}-1),

\displaystyle i,j

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{I}.

The images of
x_{i}
,
y_{i}
,
K_{i}
and
L_{i}
in
U_{\mathbf{q}}
will again be denoted by the same symbols. Let

E_{i}:=x_{i}
,
F_{i}:=y_{i}L_{i}^{-1}
in
U_{\mathbf{q}}
,
i\in\mathbb{I}
.
Then for all
i,j\in\mathbb{I}
we have

(4.4)

\displaystyle K_{i}E_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{ij}E_{j}K_{i},

\displaystyle L_{i}E_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{ji}E_{j}L_{i},

(4.5)

\displaystyle K_{i}F_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{ij}^{-1}F_{j}K_{i},

\displaystyle L_{i}F_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{ji}^{-1}F_{j}L_{i},

(4.6)

\displaystyle E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}(K_{i}-L_{i}^{-1}),

(4.7)

\displaystyle\Delta(E_{i})

\displaystyle=K_{i}\otimes E_{i}+E_{i}\otimes 1,

\displaystyle\Delta(F_{i})

\displaystyle=1\otimes F_{i}+F_{i}\otimes L_{i}^{-1}.

We consider the following subalgebras of
U_{\mathbf{q}}
:

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}(\nu)[K_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in\mathbb{I}],

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}(\nu)[L_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in\mathbb{I}],\quad U_{\mathbf{q}}^{0}=\mathbb{C}(\nu)[K_{i}^{\pm 1},L_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in\mathbb{I}],

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}(\nu)\langle E_{i}:i\in\mathbb{I}\rangle,

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}(\nu)\langle F_{i}:i\in\mathbb{I}\rangle,

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\geqslant}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}(\nu)\langle E_{i},K_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in\mathbb{I}\rangle,

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\leqslant}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}(\nu)\langle F_{i},L_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in\mathbb{I}\rangle.

The multiplication map induces linear isomorphisms

(4.8)

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}\simeq U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}U_{\mathbf{q}}^{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-}\simeq U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\geqslant}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\leqslant}.

We have canonical isomorphisms of Hopf algebras

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+0}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma^{+},

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-0}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma^{-},

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{0}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma.

The algebra
U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+}
has a canonical structure of a Hopf algebra in
{}^{\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma^{+}}_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma^{+}}\mathcal{YD}
and there are
isomorphisms of (braided) Hopf algebras

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+}

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{q}},

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\geqslant}

\displaystyle\simeq U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+}\#U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+0},

see e.g.
[
6
]
for details.

Define the module
V_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}^{*}\in\mathcal{YD}^{\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma^{-}}_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma^{-}}
with basis
\{x_{i}^{*}:i\in\mathbb{I}\}
by

\displaystyle x_{j}^{*}\cdot L_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{ji}x_{j}^{*},

\displaystyle\deg x_{i}^{*}=L_{i}^{-1},

\displaystyle i,j\in\mathbb{I}.

Let
\pi^{-}:U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\leqslant}\to U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-0}
be the canonical Hopf algebra morphism; then

{}^{\operatorname{co}\pi^{-}}U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\leqslant}=U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-}
, cf.
[
6
, Corollary 3.9 (2)]
.
Hence
U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-}
has a canonical structure of a Hopf algebra in
\mathcal{YD}^{\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma^{-}}_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)\Gamma^{-}}
.
By Remark
3.2
, we have isomorphisms of (braided) Hopf algebras

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-}

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(V_{\mathbb{C}(\nu)}^{*})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbf{q}^{(-1)}},

\displaystyle U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\leqslant}

\displaystyle\simeq U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-0}\#U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-}.

Here
\mathbf{q}^{(-1)}
means the matrix obtained by inverting every entry of
\mathbf{q}
.

Now there is a unique Hopf skew-pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\leqslant}\times U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\geqslant}\to\mathbb{C}(\nu)
determined by

\displaystyle\langle L_{i},K_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{ji}^{-1},

\displaystyle\langle F_{i},E_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij},

\displaystyle\langle L_{i},E_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle F_{i},K_{j}\rangle=0,

\displaystyle i,j

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{I},

see
[
6
, Theorem 3.7]
. By
[
6
, Theorem 3.11 (1)]
, we have

\displaystyle\langle x_{-}g_{-},x_{+}g_{+}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle x_{-},x_{+}\rangle\langle g_{-},g_{+}\rangle,

\displaystyle x_{\pm}

\displaystyle\in U_{\mathbf{q}}^{\pm},\;g_{\pm}\in\Gamma^{\pm}.

The restriction
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-}\times U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}(\nu)

is non-degenerate by
[
6
, Theorem 3.11 (3)]
and is a
Hopf skew-pairing of braided Hopf algebras by Lemma
3.3
.

4.3.
The large quantum group
U_{\mathfrak{q}}

Recall that
\mathfrak{q}\in\big(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\big)^{\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}}
belongs to a one parameter family
given by a matrix
\mathbf{q}
, cf. §
4.1
.

Definition 4.2
.

The
large quantum group

U_{\mathfrak{q}}
is the Drinfeld double of the bosonization
of the distinguished pre-Nichols algebra
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

The complex Hopf algebra
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
was defined in
[
9
]
for arbitrary
\mathfrak{q}
with
\dim{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}<\infty
.
Explicitly, let
W
be the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space with basis
(y_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
.
The group
\Gamma
acts on
V\oplus W
by

\displaystyle K_{i}\cdot x_{j}

\displaystyle=q_{ij}x_{j},

\displaystyle K_{i}\cdot y_{j}

\displaystyle=q_{ij}^{-1}y_{j},

\displaystyle L_{i}\cdot x_{j}

\displaystyle=q_{ji}x_{j},

\displaystyle L_{i}\cdot y_{j}

\displaystyle=q_{ji}^{-1}y_{j},

\displaystyle i,j

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{I}.

Now
V\oplus W
is
\Gamma
-graded by
(
4.3
), so
W
is a braided vector space with braiding matrix
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
with entries

q^{\prime}_{ij}=q_{ji}^{-1}
for
i,j\in\mathbb{I}
.
Recall the defining ideal
{\mathcal{I}}(V)
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Then
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
is the bosonization
T(V\oplus W)\#\mathbb{C}\Gamma
modulo the ideal
generated by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}(V),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}(W),

\displaystyle x_{i}y_{j}-q_{ij}^{-1}y_{j}x_{i}

\displaystyle-\delta_{ij}(K_{i}L_{i}-1),

\displaystyle i,j

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{I}.

The images of
x_{i}
,
y_{i}
,
K_{i}
and
L_{i}
in
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
will again be denoted by the same symbols. Let

e_{i}=x_{i}
,
f_{i}=y_{i}L_{i}^{-1}
in
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
i\in\mathbb{I}
.
Then for all
i,j\in\mathbb{I}
we have

(4.9)

\displaystyle K_{i}e_{j}

\displaystyle=q_{ij}e_{j}K_{i},

\displaystyle L_{i}e_{j}

\displaystyle=q_{ji}e_{j}L_{i},

(4.10)

\displaystyle K_{i}f_{j}

\displaystyle=q_{ij}^{-1}f_{j}K_{i},

\displaystyle L_{i}f_{j}

\displaystyle=q_{ji}^{-1}f_{j}L_{i},

(4.11)

\displaystyle e_{i}f_{j}-f_{j}e_{i}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}(K_{i}-L_{i}^{-1}),

(4.12)

\displaystyle\Delta(e_{i})

\displaystyle=K_{i}\otimes e_{i}+e_{i}\otimes 1,

\displaystyle\Delta(f_{i})

\displaystyle=1\otimes f_{i}+f_{i}\otimes L_{i}^{-1}.

We consider the following subalgebras of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
:

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}[K_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in\mathbb{I}],

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}[L_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in\mathbb{I}],

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}[K_{i}^{\pm 1},L_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in\mathbb{I}],

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}\langle e_{i}:i\in\mathbb{I}\rangle,

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}\langle f_{i}:i\in\mathbb{I}\rangle,

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}\langle e_{i},K_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in\mathbb{I}\rangle,

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}\langle f_{i},L_{i}^{\pm 1}:i\in\mathbb{I}\rangle.

Definition 4.3
.

The algebras
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}
and
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}
will be called
large quantum Borel algebras
and the algebras
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\pm}

large quantum unipotent algebras
.

The multiplication map induces the linear isomorphisms

(4.13)

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}\simeq U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{(-)}\simeq U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}.

We have canonical isomorphisms of Hopf algebras

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+0}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{C}\Gamma^{+},

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-0}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{C}\Gamma^{-},

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{C}\Gamma.

The algebra
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}
has a canonical structure of a Hopf algebra in
{}^{\mathbb{C}\Gamma^{+}}_{\mathbb{C}\Gamma^{+}}\mathcal{YD}
.
We have isomorphisms of (braided) Hopf algebras:

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}

\displaystyle\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}},

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\geqslant}

\displaystyle\simeq U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+}\#U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{+0},

see
[
9
]
. Define the module
V^{*}\in\mathcal{YD}^{\mathbb{C}\Gamma^{-}}_{\mathbb{C}\Gamma^{-}}
with basis
\{x_{i}^{*}:i\in\mathbb{I}\}
by

\displaystyle x_{j}^{*}\cdot L_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}_{ji}x_{j}^{*},

\displaystyle\deg x_{i}^{*}=L_{i}^{-1},

\displaystyle i,j\in\mathbb{I}.

Let
\pi^{-}:U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}\to U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-0}
be the canonical Hopf algebra projection;
then
{}^{\operatorname{co}\pi^{-}}U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}=U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}
as in
[
6
, Corollary 3.9 (2)]
.
Hence
U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}
is a Hopf algebra in
\mathcal{YD}^{\mathbb{C}\Gamma^{-}}_{\mathbb{C}\Gamma^{-}}
and because of the defining relations of

U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}
, it
is isomorphic to the distinguished pre-Nichols algebra of
V^{*}\in\mathcal{YD}^{\mathbb{C}\Gamma^{-}}_{\mathbb{C}\Gamma^{-}}
.
Combining the above, we get isomorphisms of (braided) Hopf algebras:

(4.14)

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}

\displaystyle\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathfrak{q}^{(-1)}},

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\leqslant}

\displaystyle\simeq U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-0}\#U_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-}.

Here, again,
\mathfrak{q}^{(-1)}
denotes the matrix obtained by inverting every entry of
\mathfrak{q}
.

4.4.
Lusztig isomorphisms and root vectors

As in
[
31
, §3]
we consider

(4.15)

\displaystyle\lambda_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{q}_{ii}^{-c_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}}}\mathbf{q}_{ij}\mathbf{q}_{ji})^{c_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}}}(-c_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}})_{\mathbf{q}_{ii}}^{!}\prod_{0\leq s<-c_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}}}(\mathbf{q}_{ii}^{s}\mathbf{q}_{ij}\mathbf{q}_{ji}-1)\in\mathbb{C}[\nu^{\pm 1}]^{\times},

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\neq j\in\mathbb{I}.

Notice that
\lambda_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}}\neq 0
by the definition (
3.9
).

By
[
31
, Proposition 6.8]
, there exist algebra isomorphisms
T_{i}^{\mathbf{q}}:U_{\rho_{i}(\mathbf{q})}\to U_{\mathbf{q}}
such that

(4.16)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} T_{i}^{\mathbf{q}}(\underline{K}_{j})&=K_{j}K_{i}^{-c_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}}};&T_{i}^{\mathbf{q}}(\underline{E}_{i})&=\begin{cases}F_{i}L_{i},&j=i,\\
(\operatorname{ad}_{c}E_{i})^{-c_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}}}E_{j},&j\neq i,\end{cases}\\
T_{i}^{\mathbf{q}}(\underline{L}_{j})&=L_{j}L_{i}^{-c_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}}};&T_{i}^{\mathbf{q}}(\underline{F}_{i})&=\begin{cases}K_{i}^{-1}E_{i},&j=i,\\
(\lambda_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}})^{-1}(\operatorname{ad}_{c}F_{i})^{-c_{ij}^{\mathbf{q}}}F_{j},&j\neq i,\end{cases}\end{aligned}

where the underlined letters denote the generators of
U_{\rho_{i}(\mathbf{q})}
.

Let
\omega_{0}^{\mathbf{q}}
be the element of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{q}}

of maximal lenght ending at
\mathbf{q}
and
\omega_{0}^{\mathbf{q}}=\sigma_{i_{1}}^{\mathbf{q}}\sigma_{i_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{\ell}}
be a reduced expression. By

[
31
, Theorem 6.20]
, we have that

(4.17)

\displaystyle E_{\beta_{k}}

\displaystyle:=T_{i_{1}}^{\mathbf{q}}\dots T_{i_{k-1}}(E_{i_{k}})\in U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+},

\displaystyle F_{\beta_{k}}

\displaystyle:=T_{i_{1}}^{\mathbf{q}}\dots T_{i_{k-1}}(F_{i_{k}})\in U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-},

\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{I}_{\ell}.

By
[
33
, Theorem 4.5]
the sets

(4.18)

\displaystyle\left\{E_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}E_{\beta_{2}}^{n_{2}}\dots E_{\beta_{\ell}}^{n_{\ell}}:0\leq n_{j}<\widetilde{N}_{\beta_{j}},j\in\mathbb{I}_{\ell}\right\},\,\left\{F_{\beta_{1}}^{m_{1}}F_{\beta_{2}}^{m_{2}}\dots F_{\beta_{\ell}}^{m_{\ell}}:0\leq m_{j}<\widetilde{N}_{\beta_{j}},j\in\mathbb{I}_{\ell}\right\}

are bases of
U_{\mathbf{q}}^{+}
and
U_{\mathbf{q}}^{-}
, respectively. Indeed, this follows from Property
(c)
in the Appendix
A
and Remark
4.1
. Thus the following set is a basis of
U_{\mathbf{q}}
:

(4.19)

\displaystyle\{E_{\beta_{1}}^{m_{1}}\dots E_{\beta_{\ell}}^{m_{\ell}}K_{1}^{a_{1}}\dots K_{\theta}^{a_{\theta}}L_{1}^{b_{1}}\dots L_{\theta}^{b_{\theta}}F_{\beta_{1}}^{n_{1}}\dots F_{\beta_{\ell}}^{n_{\ell}}:0\leq m_{j},\,n_{j}<\widetilde{N}_{\beta},\,a_{i},\,b_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

We now turn to the algebras
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
. Let
\lambda_{ij}^{\mathfrak{q}}
is defined as (
4.15
) with
\mathfrak{q}
in place of
\mathbf{q}
. Notice that
\lambda_{ij}^{\mathfrak{q}}\neq 0
by the definition (
3.9
).
By
[
9
, Proposition 10]
, there exist algebra isomorphisms
T_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}:U_{\rho_{i}(\mathfrak{q})}\to U_{\mathfrak{q}}
such that

(4.20)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} T_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\underline{K}_{j})&=K_{j}K_{i}^{-c_{ij}^{\mathfrak{q}}};&T_{i}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\underline{e}_{i})&=\begin{cases}f_{i}L_{i},&j=i,\\
